Well, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… I keep having these moments where someone’s talking right to me and I’m looking at them, but I still miss pieces of it. Like at the museum—I'm around there sometimes, and people will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I didn’t catch the middle part at all. 

Then I have to ask them to say it again, which is a little embarrassing. It’s not like I don’t care—actually I like hearing about that stuff, the art walk and all that—but my mind just sort of slips off somewhere for a bit.

I’ll be looking right at them, like when someone at the museum is talking about the next art walk, and I think I’m following… and then, I don’t know, my attention just kind of drifts. \n\nThen I realize I missed the middle part of what they said. It’s awkward because I’ll catch the beginning and the end, but not the important bit, so I have to say, “Sorry, can you run that by me again?” Happens even when I’m interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're not trying to listen to someone?\n\nPatient: Detached… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes I’m just kind of in my head, you know? Like I’ll be sitting somewhere and thinking about something else entirely, and then—well, that happens to everybody, I guess.\n\nBut feeling like I’m outside myself or the room isn’t real or anything like that… I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed that. I’d probably need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I might be thinking of something different.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were outside of your body, watching yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. Not like watching myself from across the room or floating up near the ceiling or anything. I mean, I can get lost in thought—like if I’m sitting in a waiting room and I start thinking about something completely different—but I’m still just… me, sitting there. I haven’t noticed that outside-my-body feeling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah… a bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say it ruins my whole day or anything, but it has caused problems.

I help with this museum group—well, I used to be more involved in planning, and we do these art walks, which I’ve always enjoyed. There’s something nice about getting people to notice little details in a painting or a building they’d usually walk past. Anyway, lately I think the group has sort of… quietly moved some of the planning pieces away from me. Not in a mean way, just more like they don’t quite trust me to keep track of it all.

And I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks the way I used to. That bothers me. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to feel pretty steady with that.

I don’t think so… not in the sense of suddenly realizing I’m doing something and having no idea how it began.

I mean, I’ll walk into the kitchen and then get distracted by something on the counter, and then I’m rinsing a mug or moving mail around—but usually if I think about it, I can trace it back. It’s more that I got sidetracked, not like a chunk of time disappeared.

Though now I’m thinking of this one drawer in our house that’s full of batteries and old keys and museum brochures, and I’ll open it for one thing and end up sorting it for ten minutes… but I know I opened it. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of not-remembering.

Like I’ll be sitting somewhere and thinking about something else entirely, and then—well, that happens to everybody, I guess.\n\nBut feeling like I’m outside myself or the room isn’t real or anything like that… I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed that. I’d probably need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I might be thinking of something different.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were outside of your body, watching yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. Not like watching myself from across the room or floating up near the ceiling or anything. I mean, I can get lost in thought—like if I’m sitting in a waiting room and I start thinking about something completely different—but I’m still just… me, sitting there. I haven’t noticed that outside-my-body feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened after these moments when your mind slips off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I lose the memory of what happened after, exactly. It’s more like… if I missed part of what someone said, then I just don’t have that part to remember.\n\nLike if somebody’s explaining something and I drift, I’ll remember being there and maybe the start of it, but the middle is just kind of blank because I wasn’t really taking it in. But after I notice and come back, I think I remember what happens then.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “after.”\n\nDoctor: Do these moments when your mind slips off cause you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say it ruins my whole day or anything, but it has caused problems.\n\nI help with this museum group—well, I used to be more involved in planning, and we do these art walks, which I’ve always enjoyed. There’s something nice about getting people to notice little details in a painting or a building they’d usually walk past. Anyway, lately I think the group has sort of… quietly moved some of the planning pieces away from me. Not in a mean way, just more like they don’t quite trust me to keep track of it all.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks the way I used to. That bothers me. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to feel pretty steady with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing something and not remembering how you started doing it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of suddenly realizing I’m doing something and having no idea how it began.\n\nI mean, I’ll walk into the kitchen and then get distracted by something on the counter, and then I’m rinsing a mug or moving mail around—but usually if I think about it, I can trace it back. It’s more that I got sidetracked, not like a chunk of time disappeared.\n\nThough now I’m thinking of this one drawer in our house that’s full of batteries and old keys and museum brochures, and I’ll open it for one thing and end up sorting it for ten minutes… but I know I opened it. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of not-remembering.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you are unreal or distant?\n\nPatient: Unreal or distant… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes if I’m tired in a big store or somewhere noisy, everything can feel a little… far off, but I think that’s more just me getting overwhelmed or not paying close attention.\n\nIt’s not like the room stops feeling real or people look fake or anything. I know where I am. Though museums can feel a little strange sometimes when it’s quiet and you’re staring at one painting too long—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I really have that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still fully connected to what's happening around you when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: I think so… I mean, mostly. When my mind wanders, I’m not gone from the room, if that’s what you mean. I still hear things, I know where I am.\n\nIt’s more like—well, like when someone is talking and I start noticing the pattern on the carpet or remembering I need to move a box in the hallway, and then I’ve missed part of what they said. But I’m still there. Not disconnected exactly.\n\nSorry, I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It’s more that I got sidetracked, not like a chunk of time disappeared.\n\nThough now I’m thinking of this one drawer in our house that’s full of batteries and old keys and museum brochures, and I’ll open it for one thing and end up sorting it for ten minutes… but I know I opened it. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of not-remembering.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you are unreal or distant?\n\nPatient: Unreal or distant… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes if I’m tired in a big store or somewhere noisy, everything can feel a little… far off, but I think that’s more just me getting overwhelmed or not paying close attention.\n\nIt’s not like the room stops feeling real or people look fake or anything. I know where I am. Though museums can feel a little strange sometimes when it’s quiet and you’re staring at one painting too long—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I really have that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still fully connected to what's happening around you when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: I think so… I mean, mostly. When my mind wanders, I’m not gone from the room, if that’s what you mean. I still hear things, I know where I am.\n\nIt’s more like—well, like when someone is talking and I start noticing the pattern on the carpet or remembering I need to move a box in the hallway, and then I’ve missed part of what they said. But I’m still there. Not disconnected exactly.\n\nSorry, I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're not trying to listen to someone or focus on something specific?\n\nPatient: Hmm… spaced out maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it a daze. If there’s nothing I’m supposed to be paying attention to, my thoughts sort of drift around. Like sitting in a waiting room, I might start thinking about whether I left a light on, then notice a magazine from 2019, then wonder why they still keep those around.\n\nBut I don’t feel unreal or out of it exactly. More like my brain is just wandering because there isn’t one thing holding it. I usually know what’s going on around me if I need to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… I keep having these moments where someone’s talking right to me and I’m looking at them, but I still miss pieces of it. Like at the museum—I'm around there sometimes, and people will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I didn’t catch the middle part at all. \n\nThen I have to ask them to say it again, which is a little embarrassing. It’s not like I don’t care—actually I like hearing about that stuff, the art walk and all that—but my mind just sort of slips off somewhere for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice anything in particular that happens right before your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: Usually there isn’t some big thing right before it. I’ll be looking right at them, like when someone at the museum is talking about the next art walk, and I think I’m following… and then, I don’t know, my attention just kind of drifts. \n\nThen I realize I missed the middle part of what they said. It’s awkward because I’ll catch the beginning and the end, but not the important bit, so I have to say, “Sorry, can you run that by me again?” Happens even when I’m interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're not trying to listen to someone?\n\nPatient: Detached… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I haven’t really noticed that. I’d probably need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I might be thinking of something different.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were outside of your body, watching yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. Not disconnected exactly.\n\nSorry, I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're not trying to listen to someone or focus on something specific?\n\nPatient: Hmm… spaced out maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it a daze. If there’s nothing I’m supposed to be paying attention to, my thoughts sort of drift around. Like sitting in a waiting room, I might start thinking about whether I left a light on, then notice a magazine from 2019, then wonder why they still keep those around.\n\nBut I don’t feel unreal or out of it exactly. More like my brain is just wandering because there isn’t one thing holding it. I usually know what’s going on around me if I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks that require sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I can sit down with something like a gallery packet or a set of notes and I’ll start out okay, but after a few minutes my eyes are still going over the page and I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nThen I have to go back and read the same paragraph again. Sometimes more than once. It’s annoying, because I’m technically looking right at it, you know? Like my eyes are doing the job but the rest of me wandered off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… I keep having these moments where someone’s talking right to me and I’m looking at them, but I still miss pieces of it. Like at the museum—I'm around there sometimes, and people will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I didn’t catch the middle part at all. \n\nThen I have to ask them to say it again, which is a little embarrassing. It’s not like I don’t care—actually I like hearing about that stuff, the art walk and all that—but my mind just sort of slips off somewhere for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice anything in particular that happens right before your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: Usually there isn’t some big thing right before it. I’ll be looking right at them, like when someone at the museum is talking about the next art walk, and I think I’m following… and then, I don’t know, my attention just kind of drifts. \n\nThen I realize I missed the middle part of what they said. It’s awkward because I’ll catch the beginning and the end, but not the important bit, so I have to say, “Sorry, can you run that by me again?” Happens even when I’m interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're not trying to listen to someone?\n\nPatient: Detached… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes I’m just kind of in my head, you know? Like I’ll be sitting somewhere and thinking about something else entirely, and then—well, that happens to everybody, I guess.\n\nBut feeling like I’m outside myself or the room isn’t real or anything like that… I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed that. I’d probably need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I might be thinking of something different.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were outside of your body, watching yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. Not like watching myself from across the room or floating up near the ceiling or anything. I mean, I can get lost in thought—like if I’m sitting in a waiting room and I start thinking about something completely different—but I’m still just… me, sitting there. I haven’t noticed that outside-my-body feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened after these moments when your mind slips off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I lose the memory of what happened after, exactly. It’s more like… if I missed part of what someone said, then I just don’t have that part to remember.\n\nLike if somebody’s explaining something and I drift, I’ll remember being there and maybe the start of it, but the middle is just kind of blank because I wasn’t really taking it in. But after I notice and come back, I think I remember what happens then.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “after.”\n\nDoctor: Do these moments when your mind slips off cause you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say it ruins my whole day or anything, but it has caused problems.\n\nI help with this museum group—well, I used to be more involved in planning, and we do these art walks, which I’ve always enjoyed. There’s something nice about getting people to notice little details in a painting or a building they’d usually walk past. Anyway, lately I think the group has sort of… quietly moved some of the planning pieces away from me. Not in a mean way, just more like they don’t quite trust me to keep track of it all.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks the way I used to. That bothers me. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to feel pretty steady with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing something and not remembering how you started doing it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of suddenly realizing I’m doing something and having no idea how it began.\n\nI mean, I’ll walk into the kitchen and then get distracted by something on the counter, and then I’m rinsing a mug or moving mail around—but usually if I think about it, I can trace it back. It’s more that I got sidetracked, not like a chunk of time disappeared.\n\nThough now I’m thinking of this one drawer in our house that’s full of batteries and old keys and museum brochures, and I’ll open it for one thing and end up sorting it for ten minutes… but I know I opened it. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of not-remembering.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you are unreal or distant?\n\nPatient: Unreal or distant… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes if I’m tired in a big store or somewhere noisy, everything can feel a little… far off, but I think that’s more just me getting overwhelmed or not paying close attention.\n\nIt’s not like the room stops feeling real or people look fake or anything. I know where I am. Though museums can feel a little strange sometimes when it’s quiet and you’re staring at one painting too long—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I really have that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still fully connected to what's happening around you when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: I think so… I mean, mostly. When my mind wanders, I’m not gone from the room, if that’s what you mean. I still hear things, I know where I am.\n\nIt’s more like—well, like when someone is talking and I start noticing the pattern on the carpet or remembering I need to move a box in the hallway, and then I’ve missed part of what they said. Not disconnected exactly.\n\nSorry, I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're not trying to listen to someone or focus on something specific?\n\nPatient: Hmm… spaced out maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it a daze. I’m not sure… I mean, I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what was I coming in here for?” But as far as doing a whole thing and not remembering how I did it, I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF44.8, F88\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… I keep having these moments where someone’s talking right to me and I’m looking at them, but I still miss pieces of it. Like at the museum—I'm around there sometimes, and people will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I didn’t catch the middle part at all. \n\nThen I have to ask them to say it again, which is a little embarrassing. It’s not like I don’t care—actually I like hearing about that stuff, the art walk and all that—but my mind just sort of slips off somewhere for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice anything in particular that happens right before your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: Usually there isn’t some big thing right before it. I’ll be looking right at them, like when someone at the museum is talking about the next art walk, and I think I’m following… and then, I don’t know, my attention just kind of drifts. \n\nThen I realize I missed the middle part of what they said. It’s awkward because I’ll catch the beginning and the end, but not the important bit, so I have to say, “Sorry, can you run that by me again?” Happens even when I’m interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're not trying to listen to someone?\n\nPatient: Detached… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes I’m just kind of in my head, you know? Like I’ll be sitting somewhere and thinking about something else entirely, and then—well, that happens to everybody, I guess.\n\nBut feeling like I’m outside myself or the room isn’t real or anything like that… I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed that. I’d probably need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I might be thinking of something different.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were outside of your body, watching yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. Not like watching myself from across the room or floating up near the ceiling or anything. I mean, I can get lost in thought—like if I’m sitting in a waiting room and I start thinking about something completely different—but I’m still just… me, sitting there. I haven’t noticed that outside-my-body feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened after these moments when your mind slips off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I lose the memory of what happened after, exactly. It’s more like… if I missed part of what someone said, then I just don’t have that part to remember.\n\nLike if somebody’s explaining something and I drift, I’ll remember being there and maybe the start of it, but the middle is just kind of blank because I wasn’t really taking it in. But after I notice and come back, I think I remember what happens then.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “after.”\n\nDoctor: Do these moments when your mind slips off cause you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say it ruins my whole day or anything, but it has caused problems.\n\nI help with this museum group—well, I used to be more involved in planning, and we do these art walks, which I’ve always enjoyed. There’s something nice about getting people to notice little details in a painting or a building they’d usually walk past. Anyway, lately I think the group has sort of… quietly moved some of the planning pieces away from me. Not in a mean way, just more like they don’t quite trust me to keep track of it all.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks the way I used to. That bothers me. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to feel pretty steady with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing something and not remembering how you started doing it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of suddenly realizing I’m doing something and having no idea how it began.\n\nI mean, I’ll walk into the kitchen and then get distracted by something on the counter, and then I’m rinsing a mug or moving mail around—but usually if I think about it, I can trace it back. It’s more that I got sidetracked, not like a chunk of time disappeared.\n\nThough now I’m thinking of this one drawer in our house that’s full of batteries and old keys and museum brochures, and I’ll open it for one thing and end up sorting it for ten minutes… but I know I opened it. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of not-remembering.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you are unreal or distant?\n\nPatient: Unreal or distant… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes if I’m tired in a big store or somewhere noisy, everything can feel a little… far off, but I think that’s more just me getting overwhelmed or not paying close attention.\n\nIt’s not like the room stops feeling real or people look fake or anything. I know where I am. Though museums can feel a little strange sometimes when it’s quiet and you’re staring at one painting too long—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I really have that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still fully connected to what's happening around you when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: I think so… I mean, mostly. When my mind wanders, I’m not gone from the room, if that’s what you mean. I still hear things, I know where I am.\n\nIt’s more like—well, like when someone is talking and I start noticing the pattern on the carpet or remembering I need to move a box in the hallway, and then I’ve missed part of what they said. But I’m still there. Not disconnected exactly.\n\nSorry, I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're not trying to listen to someone or focus on something specific?\n\nPatient: Hmm… spaced out maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it a daze. If there’s nothing I’m supposed to be paying attention to, my thoughts sort of drift around. Like sitting in a waiting room, I might start thinking about whether I left a light on, then notice a magazine from 2019, then wonder why they still keep those around.\n\nBut I don’t feel unreal or out of it exactly. I usually know what’s going on around me if I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks that require sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I can sit down with something like a gallery packet or a set of notes and I’ll start out okay, but after a few minutes my eyes are still going over the page and I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nThen I have to go back and read the same paragraph again. Sometimes more than once. It’s annoying, because I’m technically looking right at it, you know? I haven’t noticed anything physical like that. It’s more just that my attention sort of… slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having done something without remembering the steps you took to do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what was I coming in here for?” But as far as doing a whole thing and not remembering how I did it, I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nMaybe little automatic things, like making coffee—well, I don’t make it fancy, just the regular way—but I might not remember every step because I’ve done it so many times. Like my eyes are doing the job but the rest of me wandered off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like numbness or tingling, when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not numbness or tingling, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I sit funny my foot falls asleep, but that’s just—well, that’s different. Like if I’m in one of those hard chairs too long, especially at an office, and then you stand up and realize your leg’s gone all pins and needles. But when my mind wanders? I haven’t noticed anything physical like that. It’s more just that my attention sort of… slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having done something without remembering the steps you took to do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what was I coming in here for?” But as far as doing a whole thing and not remembering how I did it, I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nMaybe little automatic things, like making coffee—well, I don’t make it fancy, just the regular way—but I might not remember every step because I’ve done it so many times. But I don’t think I’m losing chunks of time or anything like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, I don’t think so. Not like I’m outside my body watching myself, no.

I mean, I’ve heard people describe that sort of thing before—like in movies or when they talk about a bad accident or something—but for me it’s more ordinary than that.     "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF44.8, F88\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… I keep having these moments where someone’s talking right to me and I’m looking at them, but I still miss pieces of it. Like at the museum—I'm around there sometimes, and people will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I didn’t catch the middle part at all. \n\nThen I have to ask them to say it again, which is a little embarrassing. It’s not like I don’t care—actually I like hearing about that stuff, the art walk and all that—but my mind just sort of slips off somewhere for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice anything in particular that happens right before your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: Usually there isn’t some big thing right before it. I’ll be looking right at them, like when someone at the museum is talking about the next art walk, and I think I’m following… and then, I don’t know, my attention just kind of drifts. \n\nThen I realize I missed the middle part of what they said. It’s awkward because I’ll catch the beginning and the end, but not the important bit, so I have to say, “Sorry, can you run that by me again?” Happens even when I’m interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're not trying to listen to someone?\n\nPatient: Detached… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes I’m just kind of in my head, you know? Like I’ll be sitting somewhere and thinking about something else entirely, and then—well, that happens to everybody, I guess.\n\nBut feeling like I’m outside myself or the room isn’t real or anything like that… I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed that. I’d probably need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I might be thinking of something different.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were outside of your body, watching yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. Not like watching myself from across the room or floating up near the ceiling or anything. I mean, I can get lost in thought—like if I’m sitting in a waiting room and I start thinking about something completely different—but I’m still just… me, sitting there. I haven’t noticed that outside-my-body feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened after these moments when your mind slips off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I lose the memory of what happened after, exactly. It’s more like… if I missed part of what someone said, then I just don’t have that part to remember.\n\nLike if somebody’s explaining something and I drift, I’ll remember being there and maybe the start of it, but the middle is just kind of blank because I wasn’t really taking it in. But after I notice and come back, I think I remember what happens then.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “after.”\n\nDoctor: Do these moments when your mind slips off cause you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say it ruins my whole day or anything, but it has caused problems.\n\nI help with this museum group—well, I used to be more involved in planning, and we do these art walks, which I’ve always enjoyed. There’s something nice about getting people to notice little details in a painting or a building they’d usually walk past. Anyway, lately I think the group has sort of… quietly moved some of the planning pieces away from me. Not in a mean way, just more like they don’t quite trust me to keep track of it all.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks the way I used to. That bothers me. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to feel pretty steady with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing something and not remembering how you started doing it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of suddenly realizing I’m doing something and having no idea how it began.\n\nI mean, I’ll walk into the kitchen and then get distracted by something on the counter, and then I’m rinsing a mug or moving mail around—but usually if I think about it, I can trace it back. It’s more that I got sidetracked, not like a chunk of time disappeared.\n\nThough now I’m thinking of this one drawer in our house that’s full of batteries and old keys and museum brochures, and I’ll open it for one thing and end up sorting it for ten minutes… but I know I opened it. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of not-remembering.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you are unreal or distant?\n\nPatient: Unreal or distant… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes if I’m tired in a big store or somewhere noisy, everything can feel a little… far off, but I think that’s more just me getting overwhelmed or not paying close attention.\n\nIt’s not like the room stops feeling real or people look fake or anything. I know where I am. Though museums can feel a little strange sometimes when it’s quiet and you’re staring at one painting too long—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I really have that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still fully connected to what's happening around you when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: I think so… I mean, mostly. When my mind wanders, I’m not gone from the room, if that’s what you mean. I still hear things, I know where I am.\n\nIt’s more like—well, like when someone is talking and I start noticing the pattern on the carpet or remembering I need to move a box in the hallway, and then I’ve missed part of what they said. But I’m still there. Not disconnected exactly.\n\nSorry, I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're not trying to listen to someone or focus on something specific?\n\nPatient: Hmm… spaced out maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it a daze. If there’s nothing I’m supposed to be paying attention to, my thoughts sort of drift around. Like sitting in a waiting room, I might start thinking about whether I left a light on, then notice a magazine from 2019, then wonder why they still keep those around.\n\nBut I don’t feel unreal or out of it exactly. More like my brain is just wandering because there isn’t one thing holding it. I usually know what’s going on around me if I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks that require sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I can sit down with something like a gallery packet or a set of notes and I’ll start out okay, but after a few minutes my eyes are still going over the page and I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nThen I have to go back and read the same paragraph again. Sometimes more than once. It’s annoying, because I’m technically looking right at it, you know? Like my eyes are doing the job but the rest of me wandered off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like numbness or tingling, when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not numbness or tingling, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I sit funny my foot falls asleep, but that’s just—well, that’s different. Like if I’m in one of those hard chairs too long, especially at an office, and then you stand up and realize your leg’s gone all pins and needles. But when my mind wanders? I haven’t noticed anything physical like that. It’s more just that my attention sort of… slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having done something without remembering the steps you took to do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what was I coming in here for?” But as far as doing a whole thing and not remembering how I did it, I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nMaybe little automatic things, like making coffee—well, I don’t make it fancy, just the regular way—but I might not remember every step because I’ve done it so many times. But I don’t think I’m losing chunks of time or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body when your mind slips off?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not like I’m outside my body watching myself, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve heard people describe that sort of thing before—like in movies or when they talk about a bad accident or something—but for me it’s more ordinary than that. I’m just sitting there and then suddenly I realize I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, whether I paid the electric bill or what I need from the store. I’m still very much in my body, unfortunately, usually in the same chair with my back getting stiff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

If there’s nothing I’m supposed to be paying attention to, my thoughts sort of drift around. Like sitting in a waiting room, I might start thinking about whether I left a light on, then notice a magazine from 2019, then wonder why they still keep those around.\n\nBut I don’t feel unreal or out of it exactly. More like my brain is just wandering because there isn’t one thing holding it. I usually know what’s going on around me if I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks that require sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I can sit down with something like a gallery packet or a set of notes and I’ll start out okay, but after a few minutes my eyes are still going over the page and I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nThen I have to go back and read the same paragraph again. Sometimes more than once. It’s annoying, because I’m technically looking right at it, you know? Like my eyes are doing the job but the rest of me wandered off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like numbness or tingling, when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not numbness or tingling, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I sit funny my foot falls asleep, but that’s just—well, that’s different. Like if I’m in one of those hard chairs too long, especially at an office, and then you stand up and realize your leg’s gone all pins and needles. But when my mind wanders? I haven’t noticed anything physical like that. It’s more just that my attention sort of… slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having done something without remembering the steps you took to do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what was I coming in here for?” But as far as doing a whole thing and not remembering how I did it, I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nMaybe little automatic things, like making coffee—well, I don’t make it fancy, just the regular way—but I might not remember every step because I’ve done it so many times. But I don’t think I’m losing chunks of time or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body when your mind slips off?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not like I’m outside my body watching myself, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve heard people describe that sort of thing before—like in movies or when they talk about a bad accident or something—but for me it’s more ordinary than that. I’m just sitting there and then suddenly I realize I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, whether I paid the electric bill or what I need from the store. I’m still very much in my body, unfortunately, usually in the same chair with my back getting stiff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… I keep having these moments where someone’s talking right to me and I’m looking at them, but I still miss pieces of it. Like at the museum—I'm around there sometimes, and people will be telling me about the next art walk, and I’m nodding along, and then suddenly I realize I didn’t catch the middle part at all. \n\nThen I have to ask them to say it again, which is a little embarrassing. It’s not like I don’t care—actually I like hearing about that stuff, the art walk and all that—but my mind just sort of slips off somewhere for a bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you notice anything in particular that happens right before your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: Usually there isn’t some big thing right before it. I’ll be looking right at them, like when someone at the museum is talking about the next art walk, and I think I’m following… and then, I don’t know, my attention just kind of drifts. \n\nThen I realize I missed the middle part of what they said. It’s awkward because I’ll catch the beginning and the end, but not the important bit, so I have to say, “Sorry, can you run that by me again?” Happens even when I’m interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're not trying to listen to someone?\n\nPatient: Detached… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes I’m just kind of in my head, you know? Like I’ll be sitting somewhere and thinking about something else entirely, and then—well, that happens to everybody, I guess.\n\nBut feeling like I’m outside myself or the room isn’t real or anything like that… I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed that. I’d probably need you to explain what you mean a little more, because I might be thinking of something different.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like you were outside of your body, watching yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I’ve had that. Not like watching myself from across the room or floating up near the ceiling or anything. I mean, I can get lost in thought—like if I’m sitting in a waiting room and I start thinking about something completely different—but I’m still just… me, sitting there. I haven’t noticed that outside-my-body feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things that happened after these moments when your mind slips off?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I lose the memory of what happened after, exactly. It’s more like… if I missed part of what someone said, then I just don’t have that part to remember.\n\nLike if somebody’s explaining something and I drift, I’ll remember being there and maybe the start of it, but the middle is just kind of blank because I wasn’t really taking it in. But after I notice and come back, I think I remember what happens then.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “after.”\n\nDoctor: Do these moments when your mind slips off cause you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say it ruins my whole day or anything, but it has caused problems.\n\nI help with this museum group—well, I used to be more involved in planning, and we do these art walks, which I’ve always enjoyed. There’s something nice about getting people to notice little details in a painting or a building they’d usually walk past. Anyway, lately I think the group has sort of… quietly moved some of the planning pieces away from me. Not in a mean way, just more like they don’t quite trust me to keep track of it all.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks the way I used to. That bothers me. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to feel pretty steady with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing something and not remembering how you started doing it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of suddenly realizing I’m doing something and having no idea how it began.\n\nI mean, I’ll walk into the kitchen and then get distracted by something on the counter, and then I’m rinsing a mug or moving mail around—but usually if I think about it, I can trace it back. It’s more that I got sidetracked, not like a chunk of time disappeared.\n\nThough now I’m thinking of this one drawer in our house that’s full of batteries and old keys and museum brochures, and I’ll open it for one thing and end up sorting it for ten minutes… but I know I opened it. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of not-remembering.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you are unreal or distant?\n\nPatient: Unreal or distant… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes if I’m tired in a big store or somewhere noisy, everything can feel a little… far off, but I think that’s more just me getting overwhelmed or not paying close attention.\n\nIt’s not like the room stops feeling real or people look fake or anything. I know where I am. Though museums can feel a little strange sometimes when it’s quiet and you’re staring at one painting too long—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I really have that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still fully connected to what's happening around you when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: I think so… I mean, mostly. When my mind wanders, I’m not gone from the room, if that’s what you mean. I still hear things, I know where I am.\n\nIt’s more like—well, like when someone is talking and I start noticing the pattern on the carpet or remembering I need to move a box in the hallway, and then I’ve missed part of what they said. But I’m still there. Not disconnected exactly.\n\nSorry, I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're not trying to listen to someone or focus on something specific?\n\nPatient: Hmm… spaced out maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it a daze. Not in a mean way, just more like they don’t quite trust me to keep track of it all.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks the way I used to. That bothers me. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to feel pretty steady with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing something and not remembering how you started doing it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of suddenly realizing I’m doing something and having no idea how it began.\n\nI mean, I’ll walk into the kitchen and then get distracted by something on the counter, and then I’m rinsing a mug or moving mail around—but usually if I think about it, I can trace it back. It’s more that I got sidetracked, not like a chunk of time disappeared.\n\nThough now I’m thinking of this one drawer in our house that’s full of batteries and old keys and museum brochures, and I’ll open it for one thing and end up sorting it for ten minutes… but I know I opened it. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of not-remembering.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you are unreal or distant?\n\nPatient: Unreal or distant… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes if I’m tired in a big store or somewhere noisy, everything can feel a little… far off, but I think that’s more just me getting overwhelmed or not paying close attention.\n\nIt’s not like the room stops feeling real or people look fake or anything. I know where I am. Though museums can feel a little strange sometimes when it’s quiet and you’re staring at one painting too long—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I really have that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still fully connected to what's happening around you when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: I think so… I mean, mostly. When my mind wanders, I’m not gone from the room, if that’s what you mean. I still hear things, I know where I am.\n\nIt’s more like—well, like when someone is talking and I start noticing the pattern on the carpet or remembering I need to move a box in the hallway, and then I’ve missed part of what they said. But I’m still there. Not disconnected exactly.\n\nSorry, I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're not trying to listen to someone or focus on something specific?\n\nPatient: Hmm… spaced out maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it a daze. If there’s nothing I’m supposed to be paying attention to, my thoughts sort of drift around. Like sitting in a waiting room, I might start thinking about whether I left a light on, then notice a magazine from 2019, then wonder why they still keep those around.\n\nBut I don’t feel unreal or out of it exactly. More like my brain is just wandering because there isn’t one thing holding it. I usually know what’s going on around me if I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks that require sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I can sit down with something like a gallery packet or a set of notes and I’ll start out okay, but after a few minutes my eyes are still going over the page and I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nThen I have to go back and read the same paragraph again. Sometimes more than once. It’s annoying, because I’m technically looking right at it, you know? Like my eyes are doing the job but the rest of me wandered off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like numbness or tingling, when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not numbness or tingling, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I sit funny my foot falls asleep, but that’s just—well, that’s different. Like if I’m in one of those hard chairs too long, especially at an office, and then you stand up and realize your leg’s gone all pins and needles. But when my mind wanders? I haven’t noticed anything physical like that. It’s more just that my attention sort of… slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having done something without remembering the steps you took to do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what was I coming in here for?” But as far as doing a whole thing and not remembering how I did it, I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nMaybe little automatic things, like making coffee—well, I don’t make it fancy, just the regular way—but I might not remember every step because I’ve done it so many times. But I don’t think I’m losing chunks of time or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body when your mind slips off?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not like I’m outside my body watching myself, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve heard people describe that sort of thing before—like in movies or when they talk about a bad accident or something—but for me it’s more ordinary than that. I’m just sitting there and then suddenly I realize I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, whether I paid the electric bill or what I need from the store. I’m still very much in my body, unfortunately, usually in the same chair with my back getting stiff.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever realize after your mind slips off that you've missed more than just a part of a conversation, like a larger period of time or events?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a big stretch of time is gone or anything.\n\nI might miss what someone said for a bit, like if they’re talking and I start thinking about something else—sometimes it’s something dumb, like whether I left the porch light on, and then that makes me think of needing to replace the bulb, and then I’m off somewhere else in my head. But I usually realize pretty quick that I’ve lost the thread. I don’t think I’m missing whole events or, you know, finding out later that something happened and I had no idea.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I wouldn’t say anxious exactly… more embarrassed, I guess. Like at the museum, people will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m standing there looking at them, nodding along, and then I realize I missed the middle of what they said.

It’s awkward because I’ll catch the beginning and the end, but not the important bit, so I have to say, “Sorry, can you run that by me again?” Happens even when I’m interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're not trying to listen to someone?\n\nPatient: Detached… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I lose the memory of what happened after, exactly. It’s more like… if I missed part of what someone said, then I just don’t have that part to remember.\n\nLike if somebody’s explaining something and I drift, I’ll remember being there and maybe the start of it, but the middle is just kind of blank because I wasn’t really taking it in. But after I notice and come back, I think I remember what happens then.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “after.”\n\nDoctor: Do these moments when your mind slips off cause you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say it ruins my whole day or anything, but it has caused problems.\n\nI help with this museum group—well, I used to be more involved in planning, and we do these art walks, which I’ve always enjoyed. Not in a mean way, just more like they don’t quite trust me to keep track of it all.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks the way I used to. That bothers me. I mean, sometimes if I’m tired in a big store or somewhere noisy, everything can feel a little… far off, but I think that’s more just me getting overwhelmed or not paying close attention.\n\nIt’s not like the room stops feeling real or people look fake or anything. I know where I am. Though museums can feel a little strange sometimes when it’s quiet and you’re staring at one painting too long—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I really have that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still fully connected to what's happening around you when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: I think so… I mean, mostly. When my mind wanders, I’m not gone from the room, if that’s what you mean. I still hear things, I know where I am.\n\nIt’s more like—well, like when someone is talking and I start noticing the pattern on the carpet or remembering I need to move a box in the hallway, and then I’ve missed part of what they said. But I’m still there. Not disconnected exactly.\n\nSorry, I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're not trying to listen to someone or focus on something specific?\n\nPatient: Hmm… spaced out maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it a daze. If there’s nothing I’m supposed to be paying attention to, my thoughts sort of drift around. Like sitting in a waiting room, I might start thinking about whether I left a light on, then notice a magazine from 2019, then wonder why they still keep those around.\n\nBut I don’t feel unreal or out of it exactly. More like my brain is just wandering because there isn’t one thing holding it. I usually know what’s going on around me if I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks that require sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I can sit down with something like a gallery packet or a set of notes and I’ll start out okay, but after a few minutes my eyes are still going over the page and I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nThen I have to go back and read the same paragraph again. Sometimes more than once. It’s annoying, because I’m technically looking right at it, you know? Like my eyes are doing the job but the rest of me wandered off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like numbness or tingling, when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not numbness or tingling, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I sit funny my foot falls asleep, but that’s just—well, that’s different. Like if I’m in one of those hard chairs too long, especially at an office, and then you stand up and realize your leg’s gone all pins and needles. But when my mind wanders? I haven’t noticed anything physical like that. It’s more just that my attention sort of… slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having done something without remembering the steps you took to do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what was I coming in here for?” But as far as doing a whole thing and not remembering how I did it, I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nMaybe little automatic things, like making coffee—well, I don’t make it fancy, just the regular way—but I might not remember every step because I’ve done it so many times. But I don’t think I’m losing chunks of time or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body when your mind slips off?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I’m just sitting there and then suddenly I realize I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, whether I paid the electric bill or what I need from the store. I’m still very much in my body, unfortunately, usually in the same chair with my back getting stiff.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever realize after your mind slips off that you've missed more than just a part of a conversation, like a larger period of time or events?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a big stretch of time is gone or anything.\n\nI might miss what someone said for a bit, like if they’re talking and I start thinking about something else—sometimes it’s something dumb, like whether I left the porch light on, and then that makes me think of needing to replace the bulb, and then I’m off somewhere else in my head. I don’t think I’m missing whole events or, you know, finding out later that something happened and I had no idea.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've missed part of a conversation or can't recall what happened?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious exactly… more embarrassed, I guess. Yeah, I get a little upset, mostly because it’s embarrassing. Like I said, what really brings me in is that at the museum somebody will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them like I’m listening, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said.

Not disconnected exactly.\n\nSorry, I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're not trying to listen to someone or focus on something specific?\n\nPatient: Hmm… spaced out maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it a daze. If there’s nothing I’m supposed to be paying attention to, my thoughts sort of drift around. Like sitting in a waiting room, I might start thinking about whether I left a light on, then notice a magazine from 2019, then wonder why they still keep those around.\n\nBut I don’t feel unreal or out of it exactly. More like my brain is just wandering because there isn’t one thing holding it. I usually know what’s going on around me if I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks that require sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I can sit down with something like a gallery packet or a set of notes and I’ll start out okay, but after a few minutes my eyes are still going over the page and I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nThen I have to go back and read the same paragraph again. Sometimes more than once. It’s annoying, because I’m technically looking right at it, you know? Like my eyes are doing the job but the rest of me wandered off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like numbness or tingling, when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not numbness or tingling, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I sit funny my foot falls asleep, but that’s just—well, that’s different. Like if I’m in one of those hard chairs too long, especially at an office, and then you stand up and realize your leg’s gone all pins and needles. But when my mind wanders? I haven’t noticed anything physical like that. It’s more just that my attention sort of… slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having done something without remembering the steps you took to do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what was I coming in here for?” But as far as doing a whole thing and not remembering how I did it, I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nMaybe little automatic things, like making coffee—well, I don’t make it fancy, just the regular way—but I might not remember every step because I’ve done it so many times. But I don’t think I’m losing chunks of time or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body when your mind slips off?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not like I’m outside my body watching myself, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve heard people describe that sort of thing before—like in movies or when they talk about a bad accident or something—but for me it’s more ordinary than that. I’m just sitting there and then suddenly I realize I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, whether I paid the electric bill or what I need from the store. I’m still very much in my body, unfortunately, usually in the same chair with my back getting stiff.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever realize after your mind slips off that you've missed more than just a part of a conversation, like a larger period of time or events?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like a big stretch of time is gone or anything.\n\nI might miss what someone said for a bit, like if they’re talking and I start thinking about something else—sometimes it’s something dumb, like whether I left the porch light on, and then that makes me think of needing to replace the bulb, and then I’m off somewhere else in my head. But I usually realize pretty quick that I’ve lost the thread. I don’t think I’m missing whole events or, you know, finding out later that something happened and I had no idea.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've missed part of a conversation or can't recall what happened?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious exactly… more embarrassed, I guess. Like at the museum, people will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m standing there looking at them, nodding along, and then I realize I missed the middle of what they said.\n\nThen I have to say, “Sorry, can you run that by me again?” Which is a little awkward, especially because they weren’t across the room or anything—they were right there. And then I start thinking, well, I hope they don’t think I wasn’t interested. The art walk is actually kind of nice, they get a decent crowd if the weather cooperates… but yes, mostly I feel bad that I missed it and have to ask again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or worried when you realize you've missed something because your mind slipped off?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I get a little upset, mostly because it’s embarrassing. Like I said, what really brings me in is that at the museum somebody will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them like I’m listening, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said.\n\nThen I have to ask, “Can you run that by me again?” And I worry they’ll think I don’t care, which isn’t true. I do care. I just… my mind slips off for a bit. The art walk thing is actually important too, because there’s planning and people coming in, and if I miss something, I feel kind of foolish.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Disconnected… I’m not sure I’d call it that. When I’m alone, I might sort of sit there and realize I’ve been staring at the same spot for a while, but I don’t feel like I’m outside myself or the room isn’t real or anything like that.

I mean, sometimes if the TV’s on and I’m not really watching it, I’ll miss half the show, especially those detective ones where if you miss one clue the whole thing makes no sense… but no, I don’t think I feel disconnected from myself or my surroundings. At least not that I’ve noticed.

It’s awkward because I’ll catch the beginning and the end, but not the important bit, so I have to say, “Sorry, can you run that by me again?” Happens even when I’m interested.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're detached from yourself or your surroundings when you're not trying to listen to someone?\n\nPatient: Detached… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I don’t think I lose the memory of what happened after, exactly. It’s more like… if I missed part of what someone said, then I just don’t have that part to remember.\n\nLike if somebody’s explaining something and I drift, I’ll remember being there and maybe the start of it, but the middle is just kind of blank because I wasn’t really taking it in. But after I notice and come back, I think I remember what happens then.\n\nUnless I’m misunderstanding what you mean by “after.”\n\nDoctor: Do these moments when your mind slips off cause you any distress or problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… a bit. I mean, I wouldn’t say it ruins my whole day or anything, but it has caused problems.\n\nI help with this museum group—well, I used to be more involved in planning, and we do these art walks, which I’ve always enjoyed. Not in a mean way, just more like they don’t quite trust me to keep track of it all.\n\nAnd I’ve stopped feeling as confident leading the art walks the way I used to. That bothers me. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to feel pretty steady with that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing something and not remembering how you started doing it?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of suddenly realizing I’m doing something and having no idea how it began.\n\nI mean, I’ll walk into the kitchen and then get distracted by something on the counter, and then I’m rinsing a mug or moving mail around—but usually if I think about it, I can trace it back. It’s more that I got sidetracked, not like a chunk of time disappeared.\n\nThough now I’m thinking of this one drawer in our house that’s full of batteries and old keys and museum brochures, and I’ll open it for one thing and end up sorting it for ten minutes… but I know I opened it. So, no, I don’t think I’ve had that kind of not-remembering.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like things around you are unreal or distant?\n\nPatient: Unreal or distant… I’m not sure I’d put it that way. I mean, sometimes if I’m tired in a big store or somewhere noisy, everything can feel a little… far off, but I think that’s more just me getting overwhelmed or not paying close attention.\n\nIt’s not like the room stops feeling real or people look fake or anything. I know where I am. Though museums can feel a little strange sometimes when it’s quiet and you’re staring at one painting too long—sorry, that’s probably not what you mean.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think I really have that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're still fully connected to what's happening around you when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: I think so… I mean, mostly. When my mind wanders, I’m not gone from the room, if that’s what you mean. I still hear things, I know where I am.\n\nIt’s more like—well, like when someone is talking and I start noticing the pattern on the carpet or remembering I need to move a box in the hallway, and then I’ve missed part of what they said. But I’m still there. Not disconnected exactly.\n\nSorry, I’m not sure if that answers it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or spaced out when you're not trying to listen to someone or focus on something specific?\n\nPatient: Hmm… spaced out maybe, but I’m not sure I’d call it a daze. If there’s nothing I’m supposed to be paying attention to, my thoughts sort of drift around. Like sitting in a waiting room, I might start thinking about whether I left a light on, then notice a magazine from 2019, then wonder why they still keep those around.\n\nBut I don’t feel unreal or out of it exactly. More like my brain is just wandering because there isn’t one thing holding it. I usually know what’s going on around me if I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing on tasks that require sustained attention?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I can sit down with something like a gallery packet or a set of notes and I’ll start out okay, but after a few minutes my eyes are still going over the page and I realize my mind has gone somewhere else.\n\nThen I have to go back and read the same paragraph again. Sometimes more than once. It’s annoying, because I’m technically looking right at it, you know? Like my eyes are doing the job but the rest of me wandered off.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anything physically, like numbness or tingling, when your mind slips off like that?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not numbness or tingling, anyway.\n\nI mean, sometimes if I sit funny my foot falls asleep, but that’s just—well, that’s different. Like if I’m in one of those hard chairs too long, especially at an office, and then you stand up and realize your leg’s gone all pins and needles. But when my mind wanders? I haven’t noticed anything physical like that. It’s more just that my attention sort of… slips away.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having done something without remembering the steps you took to do it?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I do sometimes walk into a room and think, “Now what was I coming in here for?” But as far as doing a whole thing and not remembering how I did it, I don’t know that I’ve noticed that.\n\nMaybe little automatic things, like making coffee—well, I don’t make it fancy, just the regular way—but I might not remember every step because I’ve done it so many times. But I don’t think I’m losing chunks of time or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body when your mind slips off?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. Not like I’m outside my body watching myself, no.\n\nI mean, I’ve heard people describe that sort of thing before—like in movies or when they talk about a bad accident or something—but for me it’s more ordinary than that. I’m just sitting there and then suddenly I realize I’ve been thinking about, I don’t know, whether I paid the electric bill or what I need from the store. I don’t think I’m missing whole events or, you know, finding out later that something happened and I had no idea.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or upset when you realize you've missed part of a conversation or can't recall what happened?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious exactly… more embarrassed, I guess. And then I start thinking, well, I hope they don’t think I wasn’t interested. The art walk is actually kind of nice, they get a decent crowd if the weather cooperates… but yes, mostly I feel bad that I missed it and have to ask again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel upset or worried when you realize you've missed something because your mind slipped off?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I get a little upset, mostly because it’s embarrassing. I do care. I just… my mind slips off for a bit. Like I said, what really brings me in is that at the museum somebody will be talking right to me about the next art walk, and I’m looking at them like I’m listening, but then I realize I missed the middle of what they said.\n\nThen I have to ask, “Can you run that by me again?” And I worry they’ll think I don’t care, which isn’t true. I do care. I just… my mind slips off for a bit. The art walk thing is actually important too, because there’s planning and people coming in, and if I miss something, I feel kind of foolish.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel disconnected from yourself or your surroundings when you're alone?\n\nPatient: Disconnected… I’m not sure I’d call it that. When I’m alone, I might sort of sit there and realize I’ve been staring at the same spot for a while, but I don’t feel like I’m outside myself or the room isn’t real or anything like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes if the TV’s on and I’m not really watching it, I’ll miss half the show, especially those detective ones where if you miss one clue the whole thing makes no sense… but no, I don’t think I feel disconnected from myself or my surroundings. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your mind slips off because you're not interested in what's being said or because your brain just can't seem to focus on it?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s because I’m not interested. I mean, the museum stuff does interest me—well, some of it more than others, I suppose, depending on the artist or what they’re setting up for the art walk. So then I have to ask them to run it by me again. It feels more like my brain just doesn’t stay with it, not that I don’t care.
